6. Design Principles

Thirteen non-negotiable laws. Every later phase (§12–§25) cites these by name; a deviation is a design bug, not a feature. Each: rule → rationale → tradeoff/ops → failure-on-violation.

6.1 Declarative + level-triggered reconciliation

  • Rule: Controllers act on current full state (spec + observed status/cache), never on individual watch events. Reconcile reads desired (spec) and observed (status/cache), computes diff, drives toward desired. Edge-triggered logic (on Pod-deleted, do X) is forbidden.
  • Rationale: A dropped/duplicated/reordered event must be harmless — the next watch delta or periodic resync re-reads complete state and re-derives the action. Convergence depends on level, not transition.
  • Tradeoff/ops: More reads (full re-list on resync, ~every 5–10 min); higher store/cache load vs. unbounded correctness risk of event-counting. Mitigated by the SharedIndexInformer cache (reads hit memory, not orc-store).
  • Violation: A ReplicaSet that increments a replica counter on each Pod-Add event double-counts on a duplicated watch event → spawns 2N pods, or under-counts on a dropped event → fewer than N replicas forever. Level-triggered re-reads len(matching pods) each time → self-heals.

6.2 The store is the single source of truth

  • Rule: All durable cluster state lives in orc-store. Every other component (orc-apiserver, orc-scheduler, orc-controller, orc-kubelet) is a stateless cache of store state, rebuildable from a list + watch at any resourceVersion.
  • Rationale: One authority → no reconciliation of conflicting truths. A component crash loses only in-flight work, never committed state; restart = relist + rewatch.
  • Tradeoff/ops: orc-store is the cluster SPOF until the Phase-13 Raft hook (cross-link 42); every write serializes through it (write throughput ceiling ≈ store fsync rate, target ≥ 5k writes/s single-node). Reads scale via informer caches.
  • Violation: orc-scheduler keeping an in-memory "assignments" map not derived from pod.spec.node_name → on scheduler restart the map is gone, pods double-bind or starve. Deriving all decisions from store state → restart is transparent.

6.3 Optimistic concurrency, never locks

  • Rule: Every object carries resourceVersion = the store Revision at last write. An update sends the RV it read; orc-apiserver does a store Txn compare-and-set on it; mismatch → 409 Conflict; the writer re-reads fresh state and retries. No distributed locks, no leases-as-mutexes on objects.
  • Rationale: Lock-free → no lock-holder-crash deadlock, no lease-fence complexity. Conflicts are rare (single-writer-per-field, §6.6) and resolved by cheap retry on fresh state — composing naturally with idempotent reconcile (§6.5).
  • Tradeoff/ops: Write amplification under contention (retry storms on a hot object); bound with workqueue rate limiting (§6.10) + jittered backoff. Conflict rate is an SLI; sustained > 5% on one key signals a multi-writer bug.
  • Violation: Two controllers blind-put a Deployment status without RV CAS → last-writer-wins silently clobbers the other's update; observed replica count flaps. CAS forces one to 409 → re-read → merge → converge.

6.4 Watch streams are gap-free + ordered

  • Rule: A watch from resourceVersion R streams every change with Revision > R, strictly in Revision order, no gaps, no duplicates, plus periodic Bookmark(Revision). If R is compacted → 410 Gone; client must relist + rewatch. This is the cache-correctness foundation.
  • Rationale: Informer caches are only trustworthy if the delta stream is complete and monotone — a single missed delta makes a cache silently diverge from the store with no detection.
  • Tradeoff/ops: The store must retain history back to the oldest live watch's RV → bounds compaction aggressiveness (retain ≥ last 5 min or 10k revisions). Bookmarks let idle watchers advance their RV so compaction can proceed.
  • Violation: A watch that skips Revision 42 (a Pod delete) → the informer cache keeps a ghost Pod forever → the ReplicaSet controller counts it as live → never recreates the real replacement. Gap-freedom + relist-on-410 prevents permanent divergence.

6.5 Reconcilers are idempotent

  • Rule: Reconciler::reconcile(req, ctx) is safe to run any number of times for the same observed state; repeated runs converge to the same result and cause no duplicate side effects. Returns Reconcile::{Done | Requeue | RequeueAfter(d)}.
  • Rationale: The framework will call reconcile redundantly (resync, duplicate enqueue, retry after error, leader handoff). Idempotency makes redundancy free and crash-during-reconcile recoverable.
  • Tradeoff/ops: Forces create-if-absent / name-by-deterministic-key (e.g., pod name = <rs>-<hash>) instead of unconditional create; slightly more read-before-write. Enables safe at-least-once delivery everywhere → no exactly-once machinery needed.
  • Violation: A reconcile that unconditionally create_pod() → every resync spawns another pod → replica count explodes. Idempotent form: create only if observed < desired keyed by deterministic name → re-runs are no-ops.

6.6 Single writer per field

  • Rule: Each field has exactly one writing component. orc-kubelet is the sole writer of pod.status (for its node's pods); the scheduler/controllers write spec (and spec.node_name once). Controllers own their managed objects' status (e.g., ReplicaSet controller owns ReplicaSet.status).
  • Rationale: Eliminates the hardest write races at the design level — no two actors ever legitimately contend for the same field, so CAS conflicts (§6.3) are genuine bugs, not normal operation.
  • Tradeoff/ops: Cross-component coordination must route through different fields (spec → status handshake) rather than shared mutation; spec/status split is mandatory on every kind. Single-writer is an invariant validated by admission where feasible.
  • Violation: A controller also writing pod.status.phase while the kubelet does → phase oscillates Running↔Pending as the two stomp each other; readiness gating breaks. One writer per field → status is authoritative.

6.7 Everything reconciles asynchronously

  • Rule: No synchronous cross-component RPC. Components communicate only through the store: write desired state / observed state, and watch for the other side's response. No orc-scheduler → orc-kubelet direct call; the scheduler writes node_name, the kubelet watches it.
  • Rationale: Async-via-store decouples component lifecycles — any component can be down, slow, or restarting without blocking another; the store buffers the handoff. Matches the actor model in the architecture diagram (independent async actors).
  • Tradeoff/ops: Adds store round-trips to every handoff (latency = write + watch-propagation, target p99 < 200 ms end-to-end for bind→kubelet-observe). No synchronous failure to handle, but eventual consistency must be embraced (a pod is "scheduled" before the kubelet has observed it).
  • Violation: orc-controller making a blocking HTTP call into orc-kubelet to start a pod → a slow/dead node stalls the whole controller's reconcile loop → cluster-wide control-plane stall. Watch-based handoff isolates the slow node.

6.8 Ownership drives lifecycle

  • Rule: Every child carries OwnerReference{uid, kind, controller, block_owner_deletion} to its managing object. The GarbageCollector cascades deletes (owner gone → children deleted), honoring finalizers (a finalizer blocks deletion until removed). Adoption/orphan by selector + controller ownerRef. No orphans.
  • Rationale: Lifecycle is a graph derived from ownerRefs, not from imperative cleanup code in each controller — deletion correctness is centralized in the GC and uniform across kinds.
  • Tradeoff/ops: ownerRef must be set atomically at child creation; a stuck finalizer wedges deletion indefinitely (must be observable + force-removable in ops). block_owner_deletion orders teardown.
  • Violation: A ReplicaSet creating pods without ownerRefs → deleting the RS leaves pods running forever (orphaned), consuming node capacity with no controller. OwnerRef + GC reclaims them on RS delete.

6.9 Fail toward safety

  • Rule: On uncertainty, choose the option that cannot double-run a workload. Binding is exclusive — a Pod binds exactly once (CAS on spec.node_name, empty → name). A NotReady node fences: the Node-lifecycle controller only evicts (recreates elsewhere) after the lease TTL + grace expires, never on a transient blip.
  • Rationale: Two copies of a stateful pod is worse than zero — duplicate writers corrupt data. Prefer under-availability to split-execution. Liveness is inferred conservatively from the Node Lease (§ canonical: missed lease → NotReady → grace → evict).
  • Tradeoff/ops: Slower failover (must wait out grace, target node-lease TTL 10 s + 40 s grace) vs. safety. A partitioned-but-alive node keeps running its pods; the controller must not recreate until it is certain the old copy is fenced.
  • Violation: Evicting + recreating a pod the instant a lease is late (node merely GC-paused) → two live copies of the same pod → double-bind, corrupted shared storage. Grace + exclusive bind prevents the second copy.

6.10 Bounded resources + backpressure

  • Rule: Every queue, buffer, and fan-out is bounded. WorkQueues are rate-limited + de-duplicated + delayed (RateLimiter::when/forget); watch send-buffers are bounded (slow watcher → buffer fills → disconnect with 410, forcing relist, never unbounded memory growth); informer resync has jittered period.
  • Rationale: A control plane must survive overload by shedding/slowing, not by OOM. Bounded everything → predictable memory under N× expected load.
  • Tradeoff/ops: A slow consumer gets disconnected (relist cost) rather than back-pressuring the writer; tune buffer depth (target 1024 events/watch) vs. relist frequency. Workqueue depth + retry rate are core SLIs.
  • Violation: An unbounded per-watch channel + a stalled orctl client → apiserver heap grows until OOM-kill → control-plane outage from one slow reader. Bounded buffer + 410-disconnect contains the blast radius to that one client.

6.11 The node isolates untrusted workloads

  • Rule: Container images + workloads are untrusted to the node. orc-runtime confines every container with Linux namespaces (pid/net/mnt/uts/ipc/user), cgroups v2 (cpu/mem/pids limits), seccomp/capability drops, read-only rootfs where set, pivot_root into the unpacked OCI bundle. The kubelet/runtime never trusts image-supplied paths or limits.
  • Rationale: A node runs arbitrary code; isolation is the only thing between a malicious/buggy container and the host (and the other tenants' pods). This is the node-side trust boundary in the architecture diagram.
  • Tradeoff/ops: Isolation setup cost on pod start (namespace + cgroup + mount syscalls, target sandbox-create < 250 ms) and Linux-only realism (stub runtime elsewhere). cgroup limits must be enforced before the workload runs, not after.
  • Violation: Running a container in the host PID/mount namespace with no cgroup memory cap → it sees/kills host processes and OOMs the whole node, evicting every other pod. Full namespace + cgroup confinement contains it to its own sandbox.

6.12 Observable control loops

  • Rule: Every reconcile, watch, bind, and store Txn is traced + metered. Required SLIs: reconcile latency/error/requeue rate per controller, workqueue depth, watch lag (RV behind store head), 409-conflict rate, scheduler bind latency + unschedulable count, store write/fsync latency, node-lease miss count. Structured logs key on (kind, namespace, name, resourceVersion).
  • Rationale: A distributed control loop's failures are silent (a stuck reconcile, a diverged cache, a slow watch) — only metrics expose them. Convergence is provable only if you can measure lag.
  • Tradeoff/ops: Tracing overhead (target < 3% CPU) + cardinality management (per-object labels are forbidden in metrics; use per-kind). Every Phase-14 chaos/e2e assertion checks an emitted metric, not just logs.
  • Violation: A controller whose reconcile silently wedges on one object with no metric → replicas drift for hours undetected. A reconcile_errors_total{controller} + workqueue-depth alert surfaces it in seconds.

6.13 API compatibility is sacred

  • Rule: Resource objects are versioned (apiVersion in TypeMeta). Schema changes are additive only within a version (new optional fields, new enum-arm tolerance); never remove/repurpose a field or tighten validation within a version. Breaking changes require a new apiVersion + conversion. Unknown fields are preserved (round-trip safe), not dropped.
  • Rationale: orctl, controllers, and stored objects span versions during rolling upgrades; an old client/controller must keep working against a newer apiserver and vice versa. The store holds objects written by past versions forever.
  • Tradeoff/ops: Carrying deprecated fields + conversion code indefinitely; the JSON codec must tolerate-and-preserve unknown keys (no strict-deny). Defaulting is versioned. Compatibility is a Phase-14 conformance gate, not a courtesy.
  • Violation: Renaming pod.spec.containers → spec.ctrs in place → every stored Pod and every old orctl breaks on the next read; the cluster's persisted state becomes unparseable. Additive evolution + new-apiVersion-on-break keeps old objects readable.

Cross-reference map (principles → enforcing phases): §6.1/§6.5 → Phase 4–5 (§15–§16); §6.2/§6.3/§6.4 → Phase 1, 3 (§12, §14); §6.6 → Phase 7 (§18); §6.7 → all reconcile phases; §6.8 → Phase 5 GC (§16); §6.9 → Phase 6 bind (§17) + Phase 13 drain/eviction (§24); §6.10 → Phase 4 WorkQueue (§15); §6.11 → Phase 8 (§19); §6.12 → Phase 14 (§25); §6.13 → Phase 2 codec (§13) + Phase 14 conformance (§25).

7. System Invariants

Twelve LOCKED invariants. Notation: INV-n. Each: statement · why · enforced-where · violation-detection · telemetry · recovery · debug. Invariants are checked continuously (debug builds: debug_assert!; release: cheap runtime guards + audit metrics). Any violation is a P0 control-plane incident.

ID One-line statement Owning crate
INV-1 Revision strictly monotonic, globally serialized orc-store
INV-2 Watch from R: every Revision>R, in order, no gap/dup (until compaction→410) orc-store
INV-3 resourceVersion CAS rejects stale writes → 409 orc-apiserver
INV-4 reconcile idempotent + convergent orc-controller
INV-5 Pod binds to exactly one Node (write-once spec.node_name) orc-scheduler + orc-apiserver
INV-6 only owning node's kubelet writes a Pod's status orc-apiserver
INV-7 every child reachable via OwnerReferences (no orphans post-delete) orc-controller (GC)
INV-8 finalizer blocks deletion until removed orc-apiserver + GC
INV-9 ReplicaSet drives observed replicas → desired orc-controller
INV-10 unleased Node → NotReady within grace; Pods not assumed running orc-controller (Node)
INV-11 admission/validation reject malformed objects pre-store orc-apiserver
INV-12 store survives crash with consistent committed prefix (WAL replay), no torn record orc-store

7.1 INV-1 — Revision strictly monotonic + globally serialized

  • Statement: every committed mutation (put/delete/txn) assigns a unique Revision = prev+1; reads observe a total order; no two writes share a Revision; Revision never decreases or repeats across restarts.
  • Why: Revision is the global logical clock backing resourceVersion, watch ordering (INV-2), and CAS (INV-3). A reused/regressed Revision → torn watch streams, stale-read-as-fresh, split-brain.
  • Enforced: single serialized writer in orc-store — all mutations funnel through one Txn apply path holding the keyspace write-half; next_rev = current_rev.fetch_add(1) under that critical section, persisted to WAL before ACK. On restart, current_rev = max(mod_rev) from WAL replay + 1.
  • Detected: debug_assert!(new.0 == prev.0 + 1) at commit; startup check replayed_max_rev <= persisted_high_water; watch dispatcher asserts emitted Revision strictly increases; periodic audit scans max(mod_rev) == current_rev.
  • Telemetry: store_revision_current gauge; store_commit_total counter; store_revision_regression_total (must stay 0 → alert on ≥1); trace span store.commit{rev,key,op}.
  • Recovery: regression at commit = abort process (fail-stop, never serve a bad clock); on restart WAL replay re-derives authoritative current_rev. HA path: Raft log index is the serialization point (cross-link 42).
  • Debug: dump WAL with orctl debug store wal --tail; diff mod_rev per key vs commit log; check for two writers (must be exactly one apply task — inspect store_apply_task_count == 1).

7.2 INV-2 — Watch gap-freedom + ordering

  • Statement: watch(key,end,start_rev=R) emits every WatchEvent with Revision > R for the range, in strictly ascending Revision order, exactly once (no gaps, no dups), interleaved with periodic Bookmark(rev). If R < compact_rev, the stream terminates with 410 Gone.
  • Why: informer cache correctness depends on it — a dropped event leaves the SharedIndexInformer Lister permanently stale; a dup causes double-reconcile (tolerable but wasteful); out-of-order breaks resourceVersion bookkeeping.
  • Enforced: watch fan-out reads from the ordered commit log; each WatchStream tracks last_sent_rev, never skips. Backlog (history < requested R buffered) replayed first, then live tail; a bounded per-watcher channel applies backpressure (no silent drop — slow watcher is disconnected with 410-relist, never given a gap).
  • Detected: stream-side assert(event.rev() > last_sent_rev); client-side informer tracks expected_next_rev, logs WATCH_GAP if event.rev() != stored + delta; bookmark monotonicity check; property test: random put/delete sequence vs collected events = exact set, ordered.
  • Telemetry: watch_events_sent_total{kind}; watch_gap_detected_total (alert ≥1); watch_lag_revisions gauge (current_rev − watcher last_sent); watch_disconnect_total{reason=slow|compacted}; trace watch.dispatch{watcher_id,rev}.
  • Recovery: slow/lagging watcher → server disconnects, client relists at fresh RV + re-watches. 410 Gone → informer Reflector does full LIST then WATCH from the list's resourceVersion (standard relist).
  • Debug: watch_lag_revisions spiking → identify stuck watcher channel; compare client expected_next_rev vs server commit log; replay range range(key,end,rev=R..now) to reproduce the expected event set.

7.3 INV-3 — resourceVersion CAS rejects stale writes (409)

  • Statement: an update carrying resourceVersion = V succeeds only if the object's current stored mod_rev == V; otherwise the apiserver returns 409 Conflict and the object is unchanged.
  • Why: lock-free optimistic concurrency — concurrent writers to one object cannot clobber each other (lost-update prevention); foundation of single-writer field discipline (INV-5/6).
  • Enforced: orc-apiserver Registry translates update→store.txn(compares=[Compare::mod_rev(key)==V], on_ok=[Put], on_else=[]); TxnResult.succeeded==false → map to 409. Create uses Compare::create_rev(key)==0 (fails if exists → 409/AlreadyExists).
  • Detected: integration test: two clients GET same obj, both PUT → exactly one 200, one 409; debug_assert that a successful write's pre-image mod_rev == V.
  • Telemetry: apiserver_request_total{verb=update,code=409}; apiserver_cas_conflict_total{kind}; high rate → controller hot-loop / two controllers fighting → alert; trace apiserver.update{kind,rv,outcome}.
  • Recovery: caller (controller/orctl) re-GETs fresh object, re-applies its mutation on the new base, retries (bounded backoff via WorkQueue RateLimiter). Reconcile idempotence (INV-4) makes retry safe.
  • Debug: sustained 409 spike on one key → orctl get <obj> -w to see who else writes; check ownerRef + which controller's WorkQueue is requeueing; confirm controllers aren't both managing the field.

7.4 INV-4 — reconcile idempotent + convergent

  • Statement: Reconciler::reconcile is a pure function of (desired spec, observed state); applying the same spec N≥1 times drives observed→desired and reaches a fixpoint (Reconcile::Done, no further writes) when desired==observed. No dependence on event identity/order.
  • Why: level-triggered contract — missed/duplicated/reordered watch events are harmless; correctness survives controller restart, resync, replay. Edge-triggered logic is forbidden.
  • Enforced: reconcilers read from the informer cache (Lister), never the event payload; all writes are CAS upserts keyed by deterministic identity (e.g. RS-owned Pod names by ownerRef+template hash); resync re-enqueues all keys periodically to re-establish level.
  • Detected: property test (proptest) — apply spec, run reconcile to fixpoint, re-run reconcile → asserts zero writes on the second pass (idempotence); chaos test injects duplicate/out-of-order events → final state identical; metric reconcile_writes_after_fixpoint_total must be 0.
  • Telemetry: reconcile_total{controller,result=done|requeue|error}; reconcile_duration_seconds; reconcile_requeue_total; workqueue_depth{controller}; trace reconcile{controller,namespace,name,result}.
  • Recovery: transient error → Reconcile::RequeueAfter with rate-limited backoff; convergence guaranteed because each pass re-reads full state. Non-convergent oscillation (writes never stop) → detected by sustained workqueue_depth>0 + write rate → alert.
  • Debug: thrashing controller → log spec+observed diff per pass; check for two controllers owning same children; verify reconcile reads cache not event; inspect ownerRef/finalizer churn.

7.5 INV-5 — Pod binds to exactly one Node (write-once spec.node_name)

  • Statement: pod.spec.node_name transitions None → Some(node) exactly once; once set it is immutable; no two Bind operations for one Pod both succeed.
  • Why: double-scheduling → the same Pod run on two Nodes (duplicate workload, IP/volume conflict); write-once exclusivity is the scheduling safety guarantee.
  • Enforced: Binding = a CAS update (INV-3) with Compare::mod_rev; the apiserver Bind subresource additionally rejects any update where node_name is already Some (admission rule BindOnceAdmit). Scheduler picks max-score node then issues exactly one Bind.
  • Detected: admission denies rebind; property/chaos test runs two scheduler replicas → exactly one Bind 200, other 409/denied; debug_assert(old.node_name.is_none()) in bind handler.
  • Telemetry: scheduler_bind_total{result}; scheduler_rebind_rejected_total (alert ≥1); scheduler_pending_pods gauge; scheduling_latency_seconds (target p99 < 100ms at 1k pending); trace scheduler.bind{pod,node,result}.
  • Recovery: Bind 409 → scheduler returns Pod to SchedulingQueue, re-evaluates against current cache (node may now be full → reschedule elsewhere). Leader election ensures one active scheduler; rebind-reject is the backstop if two run.
  • Debug: rebind_rejected >0 → confirm single scheduler leader (leader_election_is_leader gauge); inspect which replicas issued Bind via trace; check spec.node_name history in store WAL.

7.6 INV-6 — kubelet is the sole status writer

  • Statement: for Pod P bound to Node N, only N's orc-kubelet may write P.status; controllers/scheduler write only spec; no other actor mutates status.
  • Why: single-writer-per-field eliminates status races; lets observers trust status.phase/conditions as the node's ground truth (backs INV-10, probes, readiness).
  • Enforced: apiserver status subresource (/status) admission StatusWriterAdmit checks the authenticated identity == the kubelet ServiceAccount for status.host/spec.node_name; spec writes via the main resource path strip status; CAS (INV-3) serializes the kubelet's own updates.
  • Detected: admission denies non-owning writers (returns 403); audit log of every status write with caller identity; test: controller attempts status write → 403.
  • Telemetry: apiserver_status_write_total{caller_kind}; apiserver_status_write_denied_total (alert ≥1); trace apiserver.status_update{pod,node,caller}.
  • Recovery: denied write is a no-op (object unchanged); offending controller flagged for fixing (it should write spec/conditions it owns, not pod.status). Stale status from a dead kubelet handled by INV-10.
  • Debug: unexpected status mutation → audit log caller identity; verify the node_name on the Pod matches the kubelet's node; check token projection / ServiceAccount scoping.

7.7 INV-7 — every child reachable via OwnerReferences (no orphans)

  • Statement: every controller-created object carries an OwnerReference{controller:true} to its owner; deleting an owner cascades to all descendants (transitively); no live ReplicaSet/Pod remains whose owner has been deleted (unless explicitly orphaned via --cascade=orphan).
  • Why: prevents resource leaks (zombie Pods consuming node capacity after Deployment delete); makes the ownership graph a DAG the GarbageCollector can walk.
  • Enforced: controllers stamp ownerRef on create; GarbageCollector maintains an in-memory ownership graph (built from informer caches), on owner-delete enqueues children for cascading delete; ownerRef uid must match (dangling uid → orphan candidate). DAG: block_owner_deletion enforces parent-after-child ordering via finalizer.
  • Detected: GC periodic sweep finds objects whose ownerRef uid resolves to a non-existent owner → orphan-delete or adopt; property test: create Deployment→RS→Pods, delete Deployment, assert all gone within bound; gc_orphans_found_total.
  • Telemetry: gc_orphans_found_total; gc_cascading_deletes_total; gc_dangling_ownerref_total; gc_graph_nodes gauge; trace gc.delete{owner_uid,child_kind,child}.
  • Recovery: dangling ownerRef → GC deletes the child (foreground/background cascade per policy); if owner re-created with new uid, old children are NOT adopted (uid mismatch) → cleaned up. Bounded sweep ensures eventual cleanup even if a delete-event was missed (level-triggered).
  • Debug: zombie Pods → walk metadata.ownerReferences up the chain (orctl get pod -o yaml); check GC graph for dangling uid; verify finalizer ordering didn't deadlock.

7.8 INV-8 — finalizer blocks deletion until removed

  • Statement: an object with non-empty metadata.finalizers is NOT physically deleted on DELETE; instead deletionTimestamp is set and the object persists until the last finalizer is removed; only then does the store delete the row.
  • Why: pre-deletion cleanup hooks (volume detach, dependent cleanup, GC ordering) must complete before teardown; premature deletion → orphaned external resources / data loss.
  • Enforced: apiserver DELETE on a finalizer-bearing object = an UPDATE setting deletionTimestamp (not a store delete); a finalizer controller does cleanup then CAS-removes its finalizer string; when finalizers.is_empty() && deletionTimestamp.is_some() the apiserver issues the real store.delete.
  • Detected: debug_assert(finalizers.is_empty()) immediately before physical delete; test: object with finalizer survives DELETE (still GET-able with deletionTimestamp), vanishes only after finalizer removed; apiserver_premature_delete_total must be 0.
  • Telemetry: apiserver_delete_deferred_total{kind}; finalizer_pending_seconds (histogram — stuck finalizer = long tail); apiserver_premature_delete_total (alert ≥1); trace apiserver.delete{kind,deferred,finalizers}.
  • Recovery: stuck finalizer (controller dead) → object hangs in Terminating; operator removes finalizer manually (orctl patch ... finalizers:[]) after confirming cleanup. Detected by finalizer_pending_seconds tail alert.
  • Debug: Terminating-forever object → list its finalizers, identify the owning controller, check that controller's reconcile is running/erroring; verify block_owner_deletion chain isn't cyclic.

7.9 INV-9 — ReplicaSet drives observed → desired replicas

  • Statement: for a ReplicaSet with spec.replicas = N and selector S, the controller converges the count of live, S-matching, RS-owned Pods to exactly N (create if fewer, delete if more), and reflects it in status.replicas/readyReplicas.
  • Why: the core self-healing guarantee — Pod/Node loss is automatically repaired toward desired count; backs Deployment rollouts.
  • Enforced: ReplicaSet reconciler counts matching Pods from the Lister cache; diff = N − live; diff>0 create diff Pods (with ownerRef), diff<0 delete −diff (lowest-priority first). Idempotent (INV-4): re-running with N==live = no-op.
  • Detected: property/chaos test: kill K pods → controller recreates K within bound; scale N→M → exactly |N−M| writes; metric replicaset_replicas_desired − replicaset_replicas_current should trend to 0; replicaset_convergence_lag_seconds.
  • Telemetry: replicaset_replicas{desired,current,ready} gauges; replicaset_pods_created_total/_deleted_total; replicaset_convergence_lag_seconds; trace reconcile.replicaset{name,desired,current,diff}.
  • Recovery: transient create failure (quota/scheduling) → requeue with backoff, retried until converged (level-triggered). Persistent gap (desired>current sustained) → alert (no capacity / admission reject).
  • Debug: stuck under-replicated RS → check Pod create errors (quota/admission), scheduler pending queue, node capacity; verify selector matches template labels (mismatched selector = perpetual create loop).

7.10 INV-10 — unleased Node → NotReady within grace; Pods not assumed running

  • Statement: each kubelet renews a Node Lease (TTL T_lease, default 10s, renew every T/3); if no renewal within grace T_grace (default 40s), the Node-lifecycle controller sets Node condition Ready=False (NotReady) and, after T_evict, evicts/recreates its Pods; status of Pods on a NotReady node is not trusted as Running.
  • Why: node liveness without heartbeat-spam on the API; bounds the window of stale "Running" status; enables self-healing of node failure (INV-9 recreates evicted Pods elsewhere).
  • Enforced: orc-store Lease TTL expiry deletes the lease key (emits WatchEvent::Delete); Node-lifecycle controller watches Node leases; missing lease beyond grace → CAS-update Node condition. Lease renewal = store.lease_keepalive.
  • Detected: controller compares now − lease.last_renew vs grace; test: stop a kubelet → Node NotReady within T_grace, Pods evicted within T_evict; node_lease_renew_age_seconds per node.
  • Telemetry: node_ready{node} gauge (1/0); node_lease_renew_age_seconds; node_notready_total; node_eviction_total; node_lease_keepalive_total{node}; trace node.lifecycle{node,condition,reason}.
  • Recovery: transient (kubelet restart within grace) → renews, stays Ready, no eviction. Sustained → NotReady → graceful eviction respecting PodDisruptionBudget (Phase 13); evicted Pods rescheduled by RS controller. Flapping node → backoff before re-Ready.
  • Debug: false-NotReady → check clock skew between kubelet and store, network partition to apiserver, lease TTL vs renew interval misconfig; inspect node_lease_renew_age_seconds vs grace.

7.11 INV-11 — admission/validation reject malformed objects pre-store

  • Statement: no object reaches orc-store that fails schema validation (required fields, type/format, immutable-field violations) or admission policy; rejection returns 4xx before any write.
  • Why: the store is the source of truth (INV-12) and the input to every controller; a malformed object persisted = a poison pill that crashes/wedges reconcilers cluster-wide.
  • Enforced: apiserver request pipeline = decode → validate (structural + semantic: e.g. replicas>=0, valid selector, container has image) → admit (Admission trait: defaulting, mutating, validating webhooks, ResourceQuota/LimitRange) → Registry write. Immutable fields (uid, creationTimestamp, spec.node_name once set) checked against pre-image.
  • Detected: validation rejects → 422 Unprocessable; admission denies → 403; fuzz the JSON codec + validation (Phase 14) — no malformed input should panic or pass; apiserver_validation_reject_total, apiserver_admission_deny_total.
  • Telemetry: apiserver_validation_reject_total{kind,reason}; apiserver_admission_deny_total{webhook}; apiserver_decode_error_total; trace apiserver.admit{kind,phase,outcome}.
  • Recovery: client fixes the object and resubmits; no store mutation occurred (atomicity — validate before write). Webhook timeout → fail-closed for validating, fail-open configurable for mutating (policy-driven).
  • Debug: rejected apply → read the Status reason/message; for a persisted-bad object (shouldn't happen) → audit which version/path bypassed validation; run codec fuzz corpus to reproduce.

7.12 INV-12 — crash-consistent committed prefix (WAL replay), no torn record

  • Statement: after any crash, orc-store recovers exactly the prefix of mutations whose WAL records were durably fsync'd and ACKed; no partially-written ("torn") record is ever applied; recovered state is a consistent point-in-time snapshot at some Revision.
  • Why: the durable single source of truth (PERSISTENCE BOUNDARY); a torn write or lost ACKed commit = silent data corruption or phantom rollback → cluster-wide inconsistency / split-brain.
  • Enforced: WAL append = length-prefixed record + CRC32C checksum + Revision; fsync (or fdatasync + ordered) before ACK to the writer; commit order == Revision order; checkpoint/compaction writes a snapshot atomically (write-temp + rename) then truncates WAL prefix.
  • Detected: replay verifies each record's CRC + that Revisions are contiguous; a record failing CRC or with a gap → treated as the torn tail, truncated (everything after is discarded, prefix is consistent); store_wal_replay_records, store_wal_torn_tail_total; crash-injection test (kill -9 mid-write) → replay yields a valid prefix.
  • Telemetry: store_wal_fsync_duration_seconds (target p99 < 5ms on NVMe); store_wal_replay_duration_seconds (startup); store_wal_torn_tail_total; store_commit_acked_total; store_recovered_revision gauge; trace store.wal_append{rev,bytes} / store.recover{records,last_rev}.
  • Recovery: torn tail → truncate to last valid record (writer never got ACK for the torn one, so no client believes it committed — correctness preserved). Snapshot corrupt → fall back to prior snapshot + replay WAL. HA: Raft majority commit is the durability point (cross-link 42).
  • Debug: suspected corruption → orctl debug store verify (re-CRC all records, check Revision contiguity); compare store_recovered_revision vs last client-observed resourceVersion; inspect fsync ACK ordering for a write that "disappeared".

7.13 Invariant enforcement matrix

INV Compile-time Runtime guard Test class Alert metric (threshold)
1 Revision newtype debug_assert monotonic property store_revision_regression_total ≥1
2 — last_sent_rev check property + chaos watch_gap_detected_total ≥1
3 typed RV Txn compare integration apiserver_cas_conflict_total rate
4 Reconcile enum fixpoint assert property + chaos reconcile_writes_after_fixpoint_total ≥1
5 Option<node_name> BindOnceAdmit chaos (2 schedulers) scheduler_rebind_rejected_total ≥1
6 status subresource StatusWriterAdmit integration apiserver_status_write_denied_total ≥1
7 OwnerReference GC sweep property gc_dangling_ownerref_total
8 finalizers field pre-delete assert integration apiserver_premature_delete_total ≥1
9 — diff convergence property + chaos replicaset_convergence_lag_seconds tail
10 Lease TTL grace compare chaos (kill node) node_lease_renew_age_seconds > grace
11 typed schema validate→admit gate fuzz apiserver_decode_error_total, persisted-bad=0
12 CRC record replay CRC + contiguity crash injection store_wal_torn_tail_total (expected on recover, not steady-state)

Cross-invariant dependency chain: INV-12 (durable prefix) → INV-1 (monotonic Revision) → INV-2 (gap-free watch) → INV-4 (cache-driven reconcile) + INV-3 (CAS). INV-3 underpins INV-5/6/8/9 (all single-writer CAS mutations). INV-11 gates the store so INV-12's contents are always well-formed. A break low in the chain invalidates everything above it — debug bottom-up (store first).

8. Domain Model — Resource Objects, Store Semantics, and Reconciliation

The domain model is the spine the whole system reconciles around: typed orc-api objects persisted as MVCC keys in orc-store, read/watched through orc-apiserver, and driven to convergence by level-triggered controllers. This section fixes the exact data shapes, key layout, watch/CC protocols, and reconcile contract every later phase depends on. Terms used precisely per the glossary; no synonyms.

8.1 The Object Model — TypeMeta, ObjectMeta, spec/status

Every resource is { TypeMeta, ObjectMeta, spec, status }. spec = desired state (author/controller-owned). status = observed state (controller/kubelet-owned). The two NEVER share a writer per field — single-writer-per-field is invariant (see 8.6, 8.f).

Field group Owner (writer) Mutability Notes
TypeMeta {api_version, kind} client immutable post-create routes codec + registry
ObjectMeta.name/namespace/uid apiserver (uid), client (name/ns) immutable uid minted on create; identity key
ObjectMeta.resource_version apiserver server-set every write == store mod_rev (8.2)
ObjectMeta.generation apiserver bumped on spec change only observed via status.observed_generation
ObjectMeta.labels/annotations client/controller mutable labels are selectable; annotations are not
ObjectMeta.owner_references controller set on adopt drives GC + cascade (8.g)
ObjectMeta.finalizers controller/client mutable block deletion until cleared (8.g)
ObjectMeta.deletion_timestamp apiserver set once on DELETE w/ finalizers non-null ⇒ Terminating
spec author/controller mutable (bumps generation) desired state
status controller/kubelet mutable (no generation bump) observed state

generation vs resource_version: generation increments only when spec mutates (a status write does NOT bump it); resource_version increments on EVERY write. A reconciler is "caught up" when status.observed_generation == metadata.generation.

Compact Pod (round-trips through Phase 1 smoke test):

apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: web-7f3a
  namespace: default
  uid: 4e1c-...           # server-minted
  resourceVersion: "10472" # == store mod_rev
  generation: 2
  labels: { app: web, tier: frontend }
  ownerReferences:
    - { kind: ReplicaSet, uid: a91-..., controller: true, blockOwnerDeletion: true }
  finalizers: []
spec:                      # desired — author/RS-controller owned
  nodeName: node-3         # set by orc-scheduler bind (8.e)
  restartPolicy: Always
  nodeSelector: { disktype: ssd }
  containers:
    - { name: app, image: registry/web:1.4, ports: [{containerPort: 8080}] }
status:                    # observed — sole writer = orc-kubelet on node-3
  phase: Running
  podIP: 10.42.3.17
  conditions: [{ type: Ready, status: "True" }]
  containerStatuses: [{ name: app, ready: true, restartCount: 0 }]

Compact Deployment (manages ReplicaSets, Phase 9):

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata: { name: web, namespace: default, generation: 5, resourceVersion: "10501" }
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: web } }
  strategy: { type: RollingUpdate, rollingUpdate: { maxSurge: 1, maxUnavailable: 0 } }
  template:
    metadata: { labels: { app: web, tier: frontend } }
    spec: { containers: [{ name: app, image: registry/web:1.4 }] }
status:
  observedGeneration: 5    # == generation ⇒ rollout converged
  replicas: 3
  updatedReplicas: 3
  availableReplicas: 3

INVARIANT (template-label / selector match): spec.selector MUST match spec.template.metadata.labels — else admission rejects (the Deployment/ReplicaSet would never own the Pods it creates; orphan loop). See 8.g failure path.

8.1.1 LabelSelector match semantics

LabelSelector { match_labels: BTreeMap<String,String>, match_expressions: Vec<Requirement> }. A selector matches an object iff ALL match_labels equal AND ALL match_expressions pass (logical AND across the whole selector). An empty selector {} matches everything; a null selector matches nothing.

Form Operator Match rule Example
matchLabels (implicit =) label k present AND value == v {app: web} → has app=web
matchExpressions In label k present AND value ∈ values tier In (frontend,api)
matchExpressions NotIn label k absent OR value ∉ values tier NotIn (batch)
matchExpressions Exists label key k present (values empty) app Exists
matchExpressions DoesNotExist label key k absent (values empty) canary DoesNotExist

Validation: In/NotIn require ≥1 value; Exists/DoesNotExist require 0 values — else 422. Selectors are immutable on ReplicaSet/Deployment after create (would re-partition ownership). Field selectors (e.g. spec.nodeName=node-3, metadata.namespace=default) are a fixed allowlist per kind, distinct from label selectors, used by orc-kubelet/orc-scheduler watch filters.

8.2 Store Data Model — keys, Revision, KeyValue, resourceVersion

Objects serialize (JSON codec, orc-api) to a value under a hierarchical key:

/registry/<kind-plural>/<namespace>/<name>     # namespaced
/registry/<kind-plural>/<name>                 # cluster-scoped (Node, Namespace)
e.g.  /registry/pods/default/web-7f3a
      /registry/deployments/default/web
      /registry/nodes/node-3

LIST of a namespace = range(prefix="/registry/pods/default/", end=prefix++) (right-open range; prefix++ is prefix with last byte incremented). LIST all namespaces = /registry/pods/.

Revision(i64) — a single global, monotonic logical clock for the whole store; bumped by exactly +1 on every successful mutating op (put/delete/txn). It is NOT per-key. KeyValue carries:

Field Meaning
create_rev: Revision Revision at which this key was first created (stable across updates; reset on delete+recreate)
mod_rev: Revision Revision of the LAST write to this key
version: i64 per-key write count (1 at create, +1 per put; resets on delete)
lease: Option<LeaseId> TTL owner (node heartbeat keys; 8.3)

resourceVersion == KeyValue.mod_rev (string-encoded). A list's resourceVersion = the store's current global Revision at list time (the snapshot point a subsequent watch resumes from). This dual use — per-object RV for CAS, global RV for watch resume — is the linchpin of 8.4 and 8.d.

MVCC two-write example (global revision advances; per-key version tracks):

op1: put /registry/pods/default/web  v1   → Revision 10470
     KeyValue{ create_rev:10470, mod_rev:10470, version:1 }   resourceVersion="10470"
op2: put /registry/pods/default/web  v2   → Revision 10472   (10471 was some other key)
     KeyValue{ create_rev:10470, mod_rev:10472, version:2 }   resourceVersion="10472"

create_rev pins to 10470 (identity unchanged); mod_rev advances to 10472; global Revision is shared cluster-wide so an unrelated write consumed 10471. MVCC retains old values until compact(rev) (8.3) reclaims revisions ≤ rev.

8.3 Lease, compaction, MVCC retention

  • Lease: lease_grant(ttl) → LeaseId; keys attached to a lease are auto-deleted when the lease expires without lease_keepalive. Use: Node heartbeat (8.6) and leader-election (Phase 13). Expiry emits a WatchEvent::Delete to watchers.
  • Compaction: compact(rev) discards all historical KeyValue versions with mod_rev < rev, keeping only the latest per live key. After compaction at rev_c, a watch started from any start_rev < rev_c is unservable → 410 Gone / Expired (8.4). Compaction target: keep ≥ last 1000 revisions or 10 min of history (whichever larger) to bound watcher relist storms.

8.4 Watch Protocol — list→watch, WatchEvent stream, compaction→410→relist

WatchEvent (orc-store):

pub enum WatchEvent {
    Put(KeyValue, Option<KeyValue> /*prev*/),  // create OR update; prev=None ⇒ create
    Delete(KeyValue),
    Bookmark(Revision),                        // periodic "you are caught up to R" (no object)
}

Watch contract (LOCKED — cache correctness rests on this): a watch(key, end, start_rev) streams every change with Revision > start_rev, strictly in ascending Revision order, with NO gaps and NO duplicates, interleaved with periodic Bookmark(R). The apiserver maps store Revision ↔ resourceVersion; a Bookmark lets a client advance its resume point without a real event so a relist after a quiet period resumes cheaply.

Informer init: list-then-watch (atomic handoff at the list RV):

  1. LIST → snapshot of objects + a list-level resourceVersion = R0 (= global Revision at snapshot).
  2. WATCH(start_rev = R0) → resume EXACTLY after R0. No gap (changes at ≤R0 are in the snapshot), no dup (changes at >R0 stream once).
client                 orc-apiserver                     orc-store
  │   GET ?watch=false      │                                │
  ├────────────────────────►│  range(prefix, rev=None)       │
  │                         ├───────────────────────────────►│
  │   200 items, RV=R0      │◄─────────── items, Rev=R0 ──────┤
  │◄────────────────────────┤                                │
  │   GET ?watch=true&rv=R0 │                                │
  ├────────────────────────►│  watch(prefix, start_rev=R0)   │
  │                         ├───────────────────────────────►│
  │  Put(web, prev=None)@R1 │◄──── WatchEvent::Put @R1 ───────┤
  │◄────────────────────────┤                                │
  │  Put(web, prev=v1)@R2   │◄──── WatchEvent::Put @R2 ───────┤
  │◄────────────────────────┤                                │
  │  Bookmark(R2)           │◄──── (periodic, ~every 1–5 min)─┤
  │◄────────────────────────┤                                │
  │        ... store compacts at R_c > R0 ...                │
  │  Put/Delete @R3 ...     │                                │
  │   410 Gone (Expired)    │  start_rev < R_c ⇒ unservable  │
  │◄────────────────────────┤                                │
  │   RELIST  GET ?watch=false ──► new RV=R4, then WATCH(R4)  │  (recover; loop restarts)
  └──────────── re-watch from R4 (gap-free again) ───────────┘

410 recovery is mandatory client logic: on 410 the Reflector drops its watch, re-LISTs (fresh RV), replaces the cache, and re-WATCHes. The SharedIndexInformer surfaces this as a full resync to handlers — harmless because reconcile is level-triggered (8.5).

8.5 Optimistic Concurrency — CAS Txn, two-writer worked example

No locks. Every update is a Txn compare-and-set on resourceVersion:

UPDATE obj (client read it at RV=R_read):
  txn(
    compares: [ Compare::ModRev(key) == R_read ],
    on_ok:    [ Op::Put(key, new_value) ],     // succeeds → Revision advances, mod_rev = new RV
    on_else:  [ ]                              // fails → 409 Conflict
  )

apiserver: on on_else (RV mismatch) returns 409 Conflict; client MUST re-GET (fresh RV), re-apply its intent, retry.

Worked race — two controllers update the same Pod's labels:

Pod web @ resourceVersion=10472 (mod_rev=10472)
  CtrlA reads RV=10472 → wants labels += {a:1}
  CtrlB reads RV=10472 → wants labels += {b:2}

  CtrlA: txn(Compare ModRev==10472, Put)  → store advances → Revision 10473, mod_rev=10473  ✔ 200 OK
  CtrlB: txn(Compare ModRev==10472, Put)  → store mod_rev is now 10473 ≠ 10472             ✘ 409 Conflict
  CtrlB: re-GET web → RV=10473 (sees {a:1}) → re-apply {b:2} on fresh state
  CtrlB: txn(Compare ModRev==10473, Put)  → Revision 10474, mod_rev=10474                   ✔ 200 OK
final labels: {a:1, b:2}   — no lost update, no lock

Single-writer-per-field reduces conflicts: scheduler writes spec.nodeName, kubelet writes status, RS-controller writes labels/ownerRefs — so genuine CAS contention is rare and bounded by retry. RV is OPAQUE to clients: never arithmetic, only equality-compare and echo-back.

8.6 Reconciliation Model — the informer pipeline + level-triggered contract

        ┌──────────────────────────── orc-controller ─────────────────────────────┐
 watch  │  Reflector ──deltas──► DeltaFIFO ──pop──► SharedIndexInformer            │
◄───────┤  (LIST+WATCH a kind,       (ordered      (apply delta → thread-safe      │
orc-api  │   relist on 410)          per-key        indexed cache = Lister)         │
server  │                            queue)              │ fan-out handlers        │
        │                                                 │ enqueue object KEY      │
        │                                                 ▼                         │
        │                         WorkQueue<Request>  (rate-limited, dedup'd,       │
        │                          delayed; RateLimiter::when/forget)               │
        │                                                 │ pop key                 │
        │                                                 ▼                         │
        │   Reconciler::reconcile(Request, &Context) ──► reads desired+observed     │
        │     FROM THE CACHE (Lister), not the event  ──► Reconcile::{Done,         │
        │                                                  Requeue, RequeueAfter}    │
        └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Level-triggered contract (LOCKED):

  1. Dequeue a Request{namespace,name} — a key, NOT an event payload (the event's data is intentionally discarded; dedup collapses N events on one key to one reconcile).
  2. Read CURRENT desired (spec from cache) + CURRENT observed (status/owned children from cache).
  3. Compute diff desired − observed.
  4. Act idempotently to close the diff (create/delete/patch). Acting on already-correct state is a no-op.
  5. Requeue: Done (drop), RequeueAfter(d) (recheck later), Requeue (retry now, rate-limited).

Properties: idempotent (re-running on the same state is a no-op), edge-triggered logic FORBIDDEN (never "on Pod-deleted do X"; instead "ensure N exist"), missed event harmless (next resync/watch re-reads full state), resync = periodic re-enqueue of all keys (default 10 min, jittered) to self-heal dropped notifications. RateLimiter::when backs off hot keys (exponential, e.g. 5 ms → 1000 ms cap); forget resets a key after a clean reconcile.

8.6.1 ReplicaSet reconcile — worked example (idempotent)

ReplicaSet web-rs: spec.replicas=3, selector={app:web}
Reconcile(req=web-rs):
  desired  = 3
  observed = Lister.pods(selector {app:web}, ownerRef=web-rs) → 2 live Pods
  diff     = 3 - 2 = +1
  act      → create 1 Pod from spec.template (labels {app:web}, ownerRef→web-rs, controller:true)
  result   → RequeueAfter? no → Done; status.replicas=3 written (status-only write, no generation bump)

Re-run immediately (informer saw the create):
  desired=3, observed=3, diff=0 → no-op → Done       # IDEMPOTENT
Scale-down (spec.replicas=2 now):
  desired=2, observed=3, diff=-1 → delete 1 Pod (newest / least-ready first by deletion-cost order) → Done

If 2 Pods crash between watch events, the next resync re-reads observed=1, diff=+2, creates 2 — convergence without ever processing a "Pod died" event.

8.7 Pod Lifecycle State Machine

PodPhase ∈ { Pending, Running, Succeeded, Failed, Unknown }. Sole status writer = the orc-kubelet on spec.nodeName (8.5). Phases are terminal-monotone: Succeeded/Failed never transition back.

From Event / condition To Writer Notes
(create) object admitted, nodeName=None Pending apiserver unscheduled
Pending scheduler binds spec.nodeName Pending scheduler still Pending until kubelet syncs
Pending image pulled, sandbox + containers started Running kubelet podIP set; Ready cond follows readiness probe
Running all containers exit 0, restartPolicy ∈ {OnFailure,Never} Succeeded kubelet terminal
Running container exits ≠0 & not restartable, OR restartPolicy=Never & failed Failed kubelet terminal
Running container exits, restartPolicy=Always Running kubelet crash backoff (8, Phase 8), phase stays Running
any non-terminal kubelet lease lost ⇒ node NotReady Unknown node-lifecycle ctrl status unreachable; later evicted
any DELETE w/ finalizers ⇒ deletion_timestamp set (unchanged phase) apiserver Terminating; kubelet runs graceful stop, then key removed

Node liveness → eviction: each kubelet renews a Node Lease (TTL, e.g. 10 s renew / 40 s expiry). Missed lease → Node-lifecycle controller marks Node NotReady; after a grace period it sets evicted Pods to Unknown/deletes them so the ReplicaSet controller replaces them elsewhere (level-triggered: diff reopens).

8.8 Malformed / Failure-Path Catalog

# Input / situation Detected by Result / error Recovery
1 UPDATE with stale resourceVersion (8.5) apiserver CAS Txn on_else 409 Conflict client re-GET fresh RV, re-apply, retry
2 WATCH from start_rev < compacted rev (8.4) store: rev gone 410 Gone / Expired Reflector relist (new RV) + re-watch
3 ReplicaSet selector matches NO template.labels admission validate (8.1) 422 Unprocessable reject create; fix template labels (else owns nothing / orphan storm)
4 ownerRef cycle (A owns B owns A) GarbageCollector graph build refuse cascade; emit Event, skip break cycle by removing one ownerRef; GC retries
5 Pod no feasible Node (all filters fail) scheduler (8.e, Phase 6) stays Pending; cond PodScheduled=False reason=Unschedulable requeue in SchedulingQueue; retried as cluster changes (new Node / freed resources)
6 finalizer never removed by its controller apiserver DELETE path object stuck Terminating (deletion_timestamp set, key persists) controller must clear finalizer; ops can force-remove finalizer to unblock GC
7 two writers on same status field violates single-writer (8.6) last-write-wins → status flapping DESIGN ERROR — only kubelet writes status; enforce by convention + admission
8 match_expressions In with empty values selector validate (8.1.1) 422 supply ≥1 value or use Exists
9 LIST resourceVersion arithmetic by client (client bug) undefined / drift RV is OPAQUE — equality-compare + echo only

Cross-references: object codec + RV semantics → Phase 2 (§13); Registry/CAS/watch endpoint → Phase 3 (§14); informer/WorkQueue/Reconciler framework → Phase 4 (§15); ReplicaSet/GC/Node-lifecycle controllers → Phase 5 (§16); scheduling Unschedulable path → Phase 6 (§17); kubelet status writer + lease → Phase 7 (§18); crash backoff → Phase 8 (§19).

9. Tech Stack ADR Table

ADRs below are LOCKED. Each row records a decision binding on all phases (§12–§25). Status: all Accepted at Phase 1 unless cross-linked to a deferred phase. Format: short clauses, "X -> Y because Z".

ADR # Decision Locked Choice Rejected Alternatives Rationale When the Rejected Choice Wins
ADR-01 Implementation language Rust, edition 2021, MSRV 1.78 Go (k8s' lang); C/C++; Python GC tail-latency under watch fan-out + nil-iface/data-race classes are exactly a control-plane's failure modes; C/C++ memory-unsafe on syscall + untrusted-object hot path; Python lacks static guarantees + GIL. Rust: memory safety on kubelet/runtime syscall path, sum types + exhaustive match for PodPhase/WatchEvent, typed store API. Go wins for teams already operating Kubernetes at scale, extending its actual apiserver or client-go ecosystem, or building controllers intended for immediate k8s-compatible deployment — Go's interface{} Reconciler ecosystem and operator-sdk toolchain are more mature for that integration surface.
ADR-02 Async runtime Tokio (multi-thread scheduler) async-std (smaller ecosystem); thread-per-conn; smol Thousands of concurrent watch streams + reconcile loops + per-pod PodWorker actors need work-stealing async; Tokio is the de-facto std with mature tokio::sync, timers, I/O. smol or async-std win when binary size and dependency count dominate (embedded targets, WASM), and the watch-fan-out scale is bounded below ~100 concurrent streams; thread-per-conn wins for CPU-bound workloads with short-lived connections where work-stealing overhead exceeds benefit.
ADR-03 Workspace structure Single Cargo workspace, nine orc-* crates (orc-api, -store, -apiserver, -controller, -scheduler, -kubelet, -runtime, -net, orctl) Monolithic crate; one crate per binary with copied model; many micro-crates Enforces layering (api/store as leaf deps; binaries on top); shared compile of orc-api/orc-store types; independent test surfaces; mirrors k8s apimachinery/client-go/component split. A monolithic crate wins during the earliest exploratory prototype phase when the component boundaries are unknown — eliminating workspace overhead is valid until the architectural seams are clear. Many micro-crates win in very large organizations with separate teams per component needing independent versioning and publish cadence.
ADR-04 Persistence store Build orc-store from scratch (MVCC KV: WAL + keyspace, Revision, Watch, Lease, Txn, compaction) Embed etcd (out-of-proc, gRPC); embed sled / RocksDB directly as the API Goal is to learn MVCC + watch + lease semantics that the whole system depends on; an opaque etcd hides Revision/watch gap-freedom. sled/RocksDB kept as a deferred backing engine behind the Store trait (ADR-05). Embedding etcd wins for production-first teams that need a battle-tested, Raft-replicated, multi-leader-capable store on day one and have no intent of learning MVCC internals. The operational cost (etcd sizing, defrag, snapshot scheduling) is paid by an existing etcd ops team.
ADR-05 Store backing engine Custom WAL + in-memory BTree index behind the Store trait RocksDB/sled as the day-1 engine Store trait is the seam; a B-tree+WAL is sufficient for single-node and fully understood. sled/RocksDB pluggable later for larger-than-RAM datasets without API change. RocksDB as the day-1 backing engine wins when the keyspace is expected to exceed available RAM immediately — RocksDB's LSM-tree with block cache handles datasets larger than memory efficiently and its mature compaction tuning removes the need to hand-implement a WAL.
ADR-06 Replication / HA topology Single-node store + a Raft replication hook (cross-link project 42); deferred to Phase 13 (§24) Immediate multi-node quorum (Raft) in Phase 1; primary/replica async Orchestration core correctness is provable single-node first; Raft is orthogonal complexity. Hook = a Store-trait-level log-replication seam so HA drops in without controller/apiserver changes. Immediate multi-node Raft in Phase 1 wins when the system will run in production before Phase 13 and a single-node data loss event is unacceptable — if business availability SLAs require HA from the first deployment, the seam-first approach defers real risk.
ADR-07 Object model representation Typed Rust structs (Pod, ReplicaSet, …) impl Object, in orc-api Go-style unstructured map[string]any; dynamic schemaless docs Sum types + struct validation catch malformed spec at compile/decode time; exhaustive match on PodPhase/strategy; no stringly-typed field access. Unstructured path reserved for the Custom-resource hook only. Unstructured/dynamic representation wins when the system must process arbitrary CRDs or unknown kinds at runtime — a schema-registry-backed dynamic object model is necessary for a full CRD operator framework where types are not known at compile time.
ADR-08 Wire serialization serde + serde_json (JSON codec in orc-api) Protobuf; CBOR/MessagePack; bincode JSON is the k8s API contract (human-debuggable, orctl apply of YAML->JSON); serde gives derive-based codec with field selectors. Protobuf reserved as a later watch-transport optimization, not the API surface. Protobuf wins for the watch-stream inner loop at very high fan-out (>10k watchers, >10k events/s) where JSON serialization overhead becomes measurable — Kubernetes itself uses protobuf for internal component communication precisely at this scale threshold.
ADR-09 resourceVersion semantics ObjectMeta.resource_version (String) == decimal of store Revision at last write Per-object monotonic counter; ETag hash; wall-clock timestamp One global logical clock unifies object RV, list RV, and watch start point; makes watch-from-RV + compaction-expiry well-defined. Hashes/timestamps cannot order writes or seed a gap-free watch. Per-object monotonic counter wins if the store shards by resource kind and there is no global Revision — in a sharded store architecture each shard has its own sequence, and a global logical clock across shards requires a coordination layer that may not be present.
ADR-10 Concurrency control Optimistic: apiserver does store Txn compare-and-set on resourceVersion; mismatch -> 409 Conflict, caller re-reads + retries Pessimistic locks; last-write-wins; row versioning in SQL No held locks across the network; conflicts rare + resolved by retry on fresh state; level-triggered reconcile makes retry cheap + safe. LWW corrupts concurrent controller writes. Pessimistic locking wins when a single hot object has many competing writers making optimistic retries prohibitively expensive — for example, a single shared quota object written by hundreds of concurrent admissions would sustain >50% conflict rate; a serialized write queue is cheaper than the retry storm.
ADR-11 Watch correctness contract Gap-free, duplicate-free, strictly Revision-ordered stream from a start Revision + periodic Bookmark; compacted-start -> 410 Gone -> relist+rewatch Best-effort event bus; at-least-once with client dedup; poll deltas Informer cache correctness depends on exact-once ordered delivery; a single gap silently corrupts every Lister. 410-on-compaction forces a safe relist. Best-effort at-least-once delivery wins when consumers are inherently idempotent at a coarser granularity (e.g., log aggregation, analytics) and the relist cost for gap recovery is prohibitive relative to occasional deduplication work; gap-freedom at scale requires storing ordered history which is expensive.
ADR-12 Watch transport HTTP/1.1 chunked transfer, newline-delimited JSON WatchEvent stream (SSE-like) over the REST endpoint gRPC server-streaming; raw WebSocket; long-poll Reuses the one REST/JSON surface + proxies/auth; trivial orctl + curl debuggability; no second IDL/codec. gRPC streaming deferred with protobuf (ADR-08) if fan-out cost demands it. gRPC server-streaming wins when the watch transport represents measurable CPU at scale (>10k watch streams, protobuf encoding), bidirectional flow control matters, or the client ecosystem is service-to-service (not human-debuggable) — Kubernetes uses HTTP/2 gRPC streaming internally for exactly this reason at cloud scale.
ADR-13 Controller cache pattern Informer: Reflector -> DeltaFIFO -> SharedIndexInformer (indexed cache + Lister) -> rate-limited WorkQueue -> Reconciler; one shared informer per type Per-controller direct API polling; per-controller independent watch Shared cache amortizes one watch across N controllers; reconcile reads cache not events (level-triggered, ADR-15); polling spikes apiserver load + loses ordering. Per-controller independent watch wins only for a single-controller system where shared-cache infrastructure complexity exceeds its benefit — a single-purpose operator managing one kind benefits from simplicity over the DeltaFIFO+informer machinery.
ADR-14 Workqueue discipline Rate-limited, de-duplicated, delayed WorkQueue<K> keyed by object key; RateLimiter with exponential backoff + forget on success Unbounded channel; immediate-retry loop; per-event goroutine Dedup collapses event storms to one reconcile per key; backoff prevents hot-looping on a failing object; delayed requeue powers RequeueAfter. Unbounded channel with immediate retry wins for low-volume event streams where queue depth is provably bounded by the upstream event rate and the cost of duplicate reconcile is zero — e.g., a simple webhook handler with one reconcile target and deterministic fast success.
ADR-15 Reconcile model Level-triggered, idempotent Reconciler::reconcile reading current desired+observed; edge-triggered logic forbidden Event-driven handlers acting on individual deltas A missed/duplicated event is harmless: next resync/watch re-reads full state -> convergence. Edge logic diverges on any lost event. Edge-triggered handlers win for streaming analytics and event-processing pipelines where each event carries unique payload (not idempotent state), order matters intrinsically, and re-reading full state per event would be prohibitively expensive — message queues (Kafka, Kinesis) are designed for this model precisely because they guarantee ordered, exactly-once delivery.
ADR-16 Ownership + GC OwnerReference on children + finalizers; GarbageCollector does cascading delete; adoption/orphan by selector + controller ownerRef Controller-driven manual child deletion; no finalizers Decouples lifecycle from any single controller's liveness; finalizer blocks delete until cleanup done; cascading delete is centralized + crash-safe. Manual child deletion wins when every owner-child relationship requires custom cleanup logic that cannot be expressed as a generic GC traversal — for example, when child deletion order must be application-determined, or when external resources (cloud LBs, DNS records) must be cleaned up with specific ordering constraints that a generic GC cannot encode.
ADR-17 Scheduler architecture Pluggable scheduling framework: FilterPlugin predicates + ScorePlugin (0..100, weighted-sum) + Binding, over a SchedulingQueue Monolithic hard-coded scheduler; first-fit only; external scheduler Filter/Score plugins compose resource-fit/nodeSelector/taints/affinity independently + are unit-testable; weighted scoring tunable; mirrors kube-scheduler framework. Monolithic first-fit wins when the cluster has homogeneous nodes, the only placement constraint is resource fit, and scheduling latency is more important than bin-packing quality — first-fit has O(1) average node-scan cost vs O(M) for exhaustive filter/score.
ADR-18 Binding exclusivity A Pod binds exactly once via a CAS write of spec.node_name; scheduler is sole writer of that field Multiple schedulers race-binding; kubelet self-assigns Single-writer per field + CAS prevents double-schedule; kubelet owns status only (ADR-19). Kubelet self-assignment wins in extreme edge deployments where the scheduler is unavailable and a local node must decide placement — Kubernetes supports this via the nodeName field set in the Pod spec at submission, effectively bypassing the scheduler for statically placed workloads.
ADR-19 pod.status writer kubelet is the SOLE writer of pod.status; scheduler/controllers write spec only Any controller writes status; shared status ownership Single-writer-per-field rule eliminates status write races without locks. Shared status ownership wins when multiple independent monitoring agents need to report status conditions on the same object and none has authoritative ground truth — for example, multi-dimensional health scoring systems where network, storage, and compute health are each reported by a separate specialist agent.
ADR-20 Container runtime interface ContainerRuntime trait (CRI-like: run_sandbox/pull_image/create/start/stop/status) in orc-runtime Hard-wire one runtime into kubelet; shell out to docker/crictl Trait seam lets kubelet target real-runc-impl on Linux + a stub on other OSes; testable via a fake impl; matches CRI's decoupling. Hard-wiring one runtime wins for a minimal embedded system where the runtime is fixed, the trait abstraction overhead is non-trivial relative to the total codebase, and no multi-runtime flexibility is ever needed — a purpose-built IoT container platform might hard-wire containerd.
ADR-21 Runtime process model Out-of-process container exec (sandbox + container as separate supervised PIDs) driven by orc-runtime in-proc to kubelet In-process (containers as kubelet threads); separate shim daemon per pod A kubelet crash must not kill running pods; out-of-proc PIDs survive + are re-adopted on restart. No extra shim daemon to keep Phase 1 simple. A separate shim daemon per pod (containerd shim v2 model) wins at production scale where kubelet upgrade without workload disruption is required — the shim isolates the pod process lifetime from both the runtime binary and the kubelet binary, enabling zero-downtime kubelet upgrades.
ADR-22 Low-level container exec Direct Linux syscalls for namespaces/cgroups v2/mounts/pivot_root in orc-runtime (runc-like) Shell out to runc/crun binary; libcontainer FFI Owning the syscall path is the project's container-isolation learning goal + avoids a runc dependency; gives precise control over namespace ordering + cgroup setup. Shelling out to runc/crun wins in production systems that have no interest in managing the syscall edge directly and want to consume upstream security patches for container-start sequences without maintaining a fork — most real orchestrators (containerd, CRI-O) use this approach.
ADR-23 cgroups version cgroups v2 only (unified hierarchy) cgroups v1; dual v1/v2 support v2 is the modern default; single unified hierarchy simplifies resource accounting; avoids v1's per-controller mount sprawl. Hosts on v1 are out of scope. cgroups v1 wins on older kernels (pre-4.15) or when running on distributions that have not migrated to unified hierarchy — RHEL 7, Ubuntu 18.04 LTS on older clouds still use v1 as default, and supporting them requires v1 or dual-mode.
ADR-24 Pod network plugin NetworkPlugin trait (CNI-like setup/teardown_pod_network); reference bridge plugin Bake one CNI in; require external CNI binary day 1 Trait isolates pod-IP allocation from kubelet; a reference plugin keeps the cluster self-contained; real CNI plugins addable later. Requiring an external CNI binary on day 1 wins for production systems that will immediately use Calico, Cilium, or Flannel — the CNI binary interface is the standard interop boundary, and building a custom NetworkPlugin trait is redundant when third-party CNI plugins already satisfy the need.
ADR-25 Service dataplane Userspace ServiceProxy (ClusterIP VIP -> EndpointSlice backends) first iptables/nftables rules; eBPF/XDP datapath day 1 Userspace proxy is portable + debuggable for correctness-first; eBPF/iptables are perf optimizations deferred until the VIP->endpoint model is proven. eBPF/XDP on day 1 wins for environments with >100k connections/s or strict latency requirements where even a single extra userspace copy is unacceptable — financial trading systems, 5G packet processing, and high-performance load balancers cannot defer the datapath optimization.
ADR-26 Secret at-rest handling Secrets stored base64 in orc-store with an envelope-encryption seam (encryption-at-rest provider hook); never logged; tmpfs projection on the node Plaintext only; mandatory KMS day 1 Provider hook allows at-rest encryption without API change; tmpfs (memory-backed) projection avoids secrets on node disk. Full KMS integration deferred. Mandatory KMS on day 1 wins when regulatory compliance (FIPS 140-2, SOC 2, PCI-DSS) requires provable encryption of secrets at rest with auditable key management — a provider hook without an actual KMS fails compliance audits; the envelope-encryption stub provides no real protection.
ADR-27 Leader election Lease-based leader election (via orc-store Lease) for orc-controller/orc-scheduler HA; single active leader Active-active controllers; external lock service (ZooKeeper) Reuses the store's Lease primitive; single-active avoids duplicate reconciles/double-schedule; no extra coordination dependency. Phase 13 (§24). Active-active controllers (sharded by resource key hash) win when reconcile throughput exceeds what a single instance can sustain — at very high object churn (>100k objects, >10k events/s) a single leader reconciler is the throughput bottleneck and sharding across N active leaders (each owning a key partition) is the only horizontal scale path.
ADR-28 Error model Typed per-crate error enums (StoreError, ReconcileError, RuntimeError, NetError) via thiserror; control loops never panic! / never unwrap on fallible paths anyhow everywhere; panic-and-restart; Box<dyn Error> A panic in a reconcile loop / kubelet PodWorker is a node-down or stuck-controller incident; typed errors force exhaustive handling + retry classification. anyhow only at binary main edges. anyhow everywhere wins for rapid prototypes and CLI tools where error display to a human is the only requirement, type-based dispatch of errors is not needed, and adding thiserror enum variants for every new failure mode slows development more than it helps.
ADR-29 Observability — tracing tracing crate + OpenTelemetry exporter; span per request/reconcile/PodWorker sync log + ad-hoc strings; slog; printf Structured spans correlate a request across apiserver->store->controller; OTel exports to standard collectors; required to debug watch fan-out + reconcile latency. log + structured fields wins for single-process tools with no distributed tracing requirement — the overhead of span context propagation and OTel export is unnecessary when all computation is in-process and correlation across services is not needed.
ADR-30 Observability — metrics Prometheus text exposition (/metrics) per binary StatsD; custom format; no metrics Prometheus is the k8s-ecosystem standard; histograms for reconcile/scheduling/watch-fanout latency + counters for conflicts/requeues feed Operational Readiness SLOs. StatsD wins in legacy infrastructure where the metrics pipeline predates Prometheus adoption and migration cost exceeds the benefit — many financial and telecom operations run StatsD+Graphite stacks that have accumulated years of dashboards and alerts not easily ported.
ADR-31 Test time control Deterministic-time test harness (injectable clock + manual Tokio time advance) Real-clock sleep in tests; flaky timeouts Lease TTL, backoff, resync, probe intervals must be tested without wall-clock waits; deterministic time makes timing tests fast + reproducible. Real-clock sleep wins only when the system's timing behavior depends on wall-clock interactions with external systems (NTP, certificate expiry, external rate limiters) that cannot be injected — testing the interaction with a real external clock source requires using the real clock.
ADR-32 Fuzzing cargo-fuzz (libFuzzer) on the orc-api JSON codec + the watch protocol decoder No fuzzing; hand-rolled random tests Untrusted client objects + the watch stream are the parsing attack surface; fuzz catches panics/OOM/round-trip violations the type system can't. Hand-rolled random tests win when the input space is small and fully enumerable — for a closed API with a fixed schema and known-valid input range, structured hand-written generators cover the space faster and with better error messages than coverage-guided fuzzing.
ADR-33 Property + e2e testing proptest for invariants (reconcile convergence, watch gap-freedom, optimistic-CC safety) + an in-process e2e harness spinning a full cluster Example-based unit tests only; external Kind/k8s e2e Properties assert the locked invariants directly across random histories; in-proc harness gives fast deterministic e2e + chaos injection (kill nodes/pods/apiserver, partition store). Example-based tests win for UI components and domain logic with human-readable fixtures where the "correct behavior" is validated by a human reading the example, not derived from an invariant — property tests require a formal oracle that not all problem domains naturally have.
ADR-34 CI pipeline GitHub Actions: fmt + clippy (deny warnings) + test + cargo-deny + fuzz-smoke + e2e on Linux Self-hosted only; no CI; CI without lint/deny gates Linux runners exercise the real namespaces/cgroups path; gated merges keep MSRV + lint + license + e2e green. Self-hosted CI wins when the build requires privileged hardware (GPU, high-memory, bare-metal cgroups) that cloud-hosted runners cannot provide — Phase 8 orc-runtime tests require real Linux namespaces/cgroups, which GitHub-hosted runners provide in privileged mode, but GPU-accelerated ML workload testing genuinely needs self-hosted hardware.
ADR-35 Dependency + supply-chain policy cargo-deny (advisories, bans, license allowlist, sources); minimal vetted dep set; MSRV 1.78 pinned in CI Unrestricted deps; no audit; floating toolchain Control-plane supply chain is security-critical; deny enforces audited, license-clean, source-pinned deps; MSRV pin prevents accidental toolchain bumps. Unrestricted deps with floating toolchain win in rapid-prototyping environments where time-to-working-prototype is the constraint and supply-chain risk is deferred until the system approaches production — the overhead of deny.toml curation is non-trivial and inappropriate for a one-week spike.
ADR-36 License Apache-2.0 MIT; GPL; proprietary Mirrors Kubernetes' Apache-2.0 (patent grant, ecosystem compatibility) for the orchestrator it is modeled on. MIT wins when the project wants maximum permissiveness with minimum legal text (no explicit patent grant clause needed, smaller companies prefer fewer obligations); GPL wins for copyleft requirements where source sharing is a strategic goal (ensuring improvements are contributed back to the commons).

Cross-cutting consequences: ADR-04/05/06 make orc-store the single durable source of truth with a swap-in HA seam. ADR-09/10/11 form the consistency triad (Revision == resourceVersion -> CAS -> gap-free watch) on which ADR-13/15 (informer + level-triggered reconcile) and thus all controllers depend. ADR-18/19 (single-writer-per-field) replace locking across the scheduler/kubelet boundary. ADR-20/21/22/23 confine OS-untrusted, syscall-heavy code to orc-runtime behind a trait. ADR-28 (never-panic control loops) + ADR-31 (deterministic time) + ADR-32/33 (fuzz/property/e2e) are the correctness backbone enforced in CI (ADR-34/35).

10. Repository Layout

Single Cargo workspace, nine orc-* crate members (§ canonical) + tooling/governance roots. Tree below is the LOCKED target; inline # comments give per-file purpose. Phase column maps each crate to its build phase. Naming: snake_case files, kebab-case crate dirs, binaries = orctl, orc-{apiserver,scheduler,controller,kubelet}.

Workspace root and governance files:

  • orchestrator/
    • Cargo.toml[workspace] members=crates/* + fuzz; shared deps, profiles, lints
    • Cargo.lockcommitted (workspace ships binaries -> reproducible builds)
    • rust-toolchain.tomlchannel="1.78" (MSRV pin), components=clippy,rustfmt
    • deny.tomlcargo-deny: license allowlist (Apache-2.0/MIT/BSD), CVE/dup ban
    • rustfmt.tomlmax_width=100, edition=2021, group_imports=StdExternalCrate
    • clippy.tomlMSRV, cognitive-complexity + disallowed-methods thresholds
    • Makefilebuild|test|lint|fuzz|bench|e2e|conformance|cluster-up targets
    • Dockerfilemulti-stage: musl static build -> distroless control-plane image
    • docker-compose.ymlmulti-node dev cluster: 1 apiserver+store, ctrl, sched, N kubelets
    • .gitignore / .dockerignore
    • README.md / CONTRIBUTING.md / CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md / SECURITY.md
    • CHANGELOG.mdKeep-a-Changelog; per-release object-schema/API deltas
    • BLUEPRINT_LOG.mdappend-only phase ledger (this blueprint's execution journal)
    • BUILD_PLAN.mdphase->PR mapping, dep order, acceptance gates per phase
    • LICENSEApache-2.0

10.1 Layout invariants

Invariant Rule Rationale
Dependency DAG orc-api depends on nothing internal; orc-store on nothing internal; every other crate may dep orc-api; apiserver->store; kubelet->runtime+net; no cycles acyclic build, fast incremental, clear ownership
No tokio in orc-api object model is sync, pure data + codec reusable by every binary + tools + fuzz without runtime
Linux-real vs stub orc-runtime/orc-net gate real syscall paths behind #[cfg(target_os="linux")], else stub.rs dev/CI on macOS; e2e syscall tests Linux-only
One binary per control-plane role main.rs per apiserver/scheduler/controller/kubelet/orctl; P0 all-in-one dev bin lives behind a --all-in-one flag in orctl or a dev example independent deploy + leader election per component
Fixtures are shared conformance/objects/* reused by fuzz seeds + e2e + unit tests one source of truth for canonical objects
Phase tags each crate/file carries [Pn] build phase (above); BUILD_PLAN.md is authoritative maps tree -> § 12–26 phases

10.2 Module-home rule

Every LOCKED core type lives in exactly one file (above): Store/Revision/KeyValue->orc-store/src/{lib,mvcc}.rs; Reconciler/WorkQueue->orc-controller/src/framework/; ContainerRuntime->orc-runtime/src/lib.rs.
